Um… I guess I’ve just been under a lot of stress lately. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but since the foreclosure notices started coming, I feel like I’m kind of coming apart.

I’ve snapped at a couple of the apprentices, and I’m usually not like that. I’m also making dumb mistakes in the repair bay—stuff I know better than to mess up. I figured I should probably talk to someone before it gets worse… if that makes sense.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

I mean… I don’t know if I’d say sad every minute of the day, but since those foreclosure notices started coming, it’s like I’ve been coming apart at the seams.

I’ll sit in my truck before work and cry, and then try to pull myself together so nobody notices. I jump when the phone rings, like it’s going to be more bad news. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but since the foreclosure notices started coming, I feel like I’m kind of coming apart.\n\nI’ve been crying in my truck before work, which is embarrassing. And every time the phone rings I jump, like it’s going to be more bad news. I’ve snapped at a couple of the apprentices, and I’m usually not like that. I’m also making dumb mistakes in the repair bay—stuff I know better than to mess up. I figured I should probably talk to someone before it gets worse… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so? Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more than I’d like to admit, I guess. I keep trying to tell myself I’m handling it, but ever since those foreclosure notices started coming, I feel like I’m just… on edge all the time.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting in my truck before work crying, which is embarrassing to even say. And if the phone rings, I jump, like it’s going to be more bad news. I’ve been snapping at the apprentices too, and I hate that because they don’t deserve it.\n\nI’ve even made some dumb mistakes in the repair bay that I normally wouldn’t make. I’m not trying to make excuses, but… yeah, I guess I do feel nervous and kind of coming apart. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d say sad every minute of the day, but since those foreclosure notices started coming, it’s like I’ve been coming apart at the seams.\n\nI’ll sit in my truck before work and cry, and then try to pull myself together so nobody notices. I jump when the phone rings, like it’s going to be more bad news. And I’ve been snapping at the apprentices, which I feel awful about, and making stupid mistakes in the repair bay that I just… I never would’ve made before.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess sad is part of it. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been a lot. Is that the kind of answer you’re looking for?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping at night?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve really paid close attention to my sleep like that.\n\nI mean, some nights are probably better than others, but I wouldn’t want to say it’s a big problem if I’m not sure. What counts as “trouble sleeping,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve really been paying attention to it that way.\n\nI mean, I still do the things I usually do, but I don’t know if I’d call it not feeling pleasure. Maybe I’m just distracted or worn out? I don’t want to overstate it if that’s not quite what you mean.\n\nCould you maybe give me an example of what you’re looking for?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like activities that normally bring you happiness no longer do?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think I can say they don’t bring me happiness anymore.\n\nI guess lately I’m more preoccupied when I’m doing things, so maybe I’m not enjoying them the same way? But I don’t want to make that sound like I’ve completely lost interest or anything, because I’m not sure that’s true.\n\nMaybe I’d have to think about it more. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve totally lost interest, no… or at least I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly.\n\nI still do some of the things I normally like. Maybe I’m not as into them sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s “lost interest” or just having a lot on my mind. I don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a significant change, no. My clothes don’t feel that different or anything… at least not enough that I’d say I’ve lost a lot of weight.\n\nI mean, I’m not tracking it closely, so I could be off, but I don’t think so.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but since the foreclosure notices started coming, I feel like I’m kind of coming apart.\n\nI’ve been crying in my truck before work, which is embarrassing. And every time the phone rings I jump, like it’s going to be more bad news. I’ve snapped at a couple of the apprentices, and I’m usually not like that. I’m also making dumb mistakes in the repair bay—stuff I know better than to mess up. I figured I should probably talk to someone before it gets worse… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so? Not like one specific thought that just repeats over and over, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I worry, obviously, but I’m not sure I’d call it recurring thoughts I can’t control. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I try to tell myself I’m just busy or tired, but I’ve kind of stopped doing the things I used to keep up with.\n\nI haven’t been going to my church volunteering, and I’ve missed my fitness classes and the truck-club outings too. And at work they’ve taken me out of the high-risk repair bay for now, which… I understand, I guess, but it doesn’t feel great.\n\nSo, no, I’m not really enjoying much the way I used to. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy that now feel pleasurable but you're just not motivated to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s pretty accurate. Like, if I actually got myself there, I’d probably still feel okay doing it, you know? Church volunteering, the fitness classes, the truck-club stuff… I used to keep up with all that.\n\nBut lately I just don’t go. I tell myself I’m tired or I’ll go next time, and then I don’t. And work’s been affected too — they’ve pulled me out of the high-risk repair bay for now. I get why, and I’m trying not to take it personally, but… it’s hard not to feel like I’m letting people down. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more than I’d like to admit, I guess. I keep trying to tell myself I’m handling it, but ever since those foreclosure notices started coming, I feel like I’m just… on edge all the time.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting in my truck before work crying, which is embarrassing to even say. And if the phone rings, I jump, like it’s going to be more bad news. I’ve been snapping at the apprentices too, and I hate that because they don’t deserve it.\n\nI’ve even made some dumb mistakes in the repair bay that I normally wouldn’t make. I’m not trying to make excuses, but… yeah, I guess I do feel nervous and kind of coming apart. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d say sad every minute of the day, but since those foreclosure notices started coming, it’s like I’ve been coming apart at the seams.\n\nI’ll sit in my truck before work and cry, and then try to pull myself together so nobody notices. I jump when the phone rings, like it’s going to be more bad news. And I’ve been snapping at the apprentices, which I feel awful about, and making stupid mistakes in the repair bay that I just… I never would’ve made before.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess sad is part of it. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been a lot. Is that the kind of answer you’re looking for?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping at night?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve really paid close attention to my sleep like that.\n\nI mean, some nights are probably better than others, but I wouldn’t want to say it’s a big problem if I’m not sure. What counts as “trouble sleeping,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve really been paying attention to it that way.\n\nI mean, I still do the things I usually do, but I don’t know if I’d call it not feeling pleasure. Maybe I’m just distracted or worn out? I don’t want to overstate it if that’s not quite what you mean.\n\nCould you maybe give me an example of what you’re looking for?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like activities that normally bring you happiness no longer do?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think I can say they don’t bring me happiness anymore.\n\nI guess lately I’m more preoccupied when I’m doing things, so maybe I’m not enjoying them the same way? But I don’t want to make that sound like I’ve completely lost interest or anything, because I’m not sure that’s true.\n\nMaybe I’d have to think about it more. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve totally lost interest, no… or at least I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly.\n\nI still do some of the things I normally like. Maybe I’m not as into them sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s “lost interest” or just having a lot on my mind. I don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a significant change, no. My clothes don’t feel that different or anything… at least not enough that I’d say I’ve lost a lot of weight.\n\nI mean, I’m not tracking it closely, so I could be off, but I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you gained a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I don’t want to say it like I know for sure… but my clothes feel about the same, and I don’t think there’s been any big weight gain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve totally lost interest, no… or at least I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly.\n\nI still do some of the things I normally like. Maybe I’m not as into them sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s “lost interest” or just having a lot on my mind. I don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a significant change, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I don’t want to say it like I know for sure… but my clothes feel about the same, and I don’t think there’s been any big weight gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say “often,” I don’t think. I’ve been snapping at the apprentices too, and I hate that because they don’t deserve it.\n\nI’ve even made some dumb mistakes in the repair bay that I normally wouldn’t make. I jump when the phone rings, like it’s going to be more bad news. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve totally lost interest, no… or at least I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly.\n\nI still do some of the things I normally like. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that you normally find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say… not really unable, no. I still can get some pleasure from things I normally like.\n\nMaybe it feels a little harder to get into them sometimes, but I don’t want to overstate it. I wouldn’t say it’s completely gone or anything. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been feeling this way, with symptoms like crying, snapping at others, and making mistakes at work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / heavy_vehicle_or_mobile_equipment_service_technician_or_mechanic / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about four months, ever since the foreclosure notices started coming in.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

It’s been… I think about four months now. Around when the foreclosure notices started coming in.

I don’t want to make it sound like I fell apart overnight, but that’s when I started noticing I wasn’t handling things as well. Does that seem like the kind of timeframe you’re asking for?

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.
